Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, hi, Doctor. Thanks for fitting me in. I, uh, I guess what brings me in today is... well, it’s this feeling I get, and it's been driving me a bit crazy lately. 

See, I work as a building cleaner—janitorial stuff, maintenance, keeping the place running. And when I’m on the clock doing my rounds, I’m okay, because I’m always moving. But the absolute second I’m supposed to just sit still, it’s like... I don't know, it’s like I’ve got this motor humming right under my skin. Just buzzing away. 

Like at work, we have these mandatory staff meetings, usually Tuesday mornings, and we're all crammed in this little break room. The supervisor is going over the supply lists or safety protocols, and I just cannot stay in my seat. I end up shifting my weight, crossing and uncrossing my legs, tapping my boots, and eventually, I just have to stand up and lean against the back wall. Or I’ll find some excuse to move around, like I'll say I need to check the mop buckets or grab another cup of coffee, just to get my legs working. 

And it’s not just at work, either. I help out a lot at my church, and whenever we have these event briefings—you know, planning the potlucks or the charity drives—we sit in those hard metal folding chairs, and it hits me again. That same humming feeling. I end up getting up to adjust the thermostat or hand out papers just so I don't have to sit there. It’s just this constant restlessness, and honestly, people are starting to look at me funny during these meetings, so I figured I better come talk to somebody about it.

Well, you know, it’s funny you ask that because my wife always asks me if I’m stressed out or anxious when I’m bouncing my leg or pacing around the living room. But honestly? No, it’s not really my mind racing at all. I mean, I’m not sitting there worrying about paying the electric bill or, uh, whether my truck needs new brake pads—even though it probably does, it's been squeaking a bit when I pull into the lot at work. But anyway, it’s not like that. My head is usually pretty quiet. 

It’s really just... it's all in the body. It’s exactly like my legs have their own separate battery pack that's fully charged, and somebody flipped the switch to 'on', but I’m trying to hold the brakes down. Like at those Tuesday meetings I was telling you about? My brain is actually just trying to listen to my supervisor. He talks a little slow, which maybe doesn't help the situation, but I’m just sitting there thinking about the normal stuff, like the floor buffing schedule for the third-floor hallway. I’m not worried about anything. But my knees are just jumping, and my feet almost feel like they’re resting on hot coals. 

It’s this physical pressure, you know? It builds up right behind my kneecaps, or down in my calves, and if I don't stretch them out or just walk it off, I feel like I’m going to literally jump right out of my own skin. So, yeah, I wouldn't say my thoughts are racing or anything like that. It’s just this overwhelming physical itch to get up and do something, anything, as long as it involves moving my feet.
=====================================

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
You know, I haven't really noticed it being tied to the clock at all. It’s a good question, though, because I actually tried to pay attention to that a while back to see if there was a pattern. 

See, my schedule is pretty regular. I’m usually up by 4:30 in the morning, I clock in by 6:00, and I hit the ground running with the main lobbies and the glass doors because that’s when the foot traffic is lowest. You gotta get the glass done before people start putting their hands all over it, you know? So during those early hours, I’m fine, perfectly fine, because I'm pushing the cart and moving. But then, say it’s around 10:00 AM and I have to sit down at the little desk in the utility closet to fill out the inventory requisition forms for the cleaning chemicals—bam, my legs start going. 

But then again, it happens in the evening, too. I get home, have dinner with my wife, and we like to sit down and watch the evening news or maybe a baseball game if it's on. I’ve got this old recliner, really comfortable, had it for maybe fifteen years. I even reupholstered it myself a few years back. You’d think I’d just melt into it after being on my feet on hard concrete and linoleum for eight hours. But nope. Even at 8:00 PM, if I’m just sitting there watching TV, after about ten or fifteen minutes, that motor starts humming again. I end up having to get up and fiddle with loading the dishwasher, or I'll take the recycling out, or I'll just stand and pace behind the couch while I watch the rest of the game. 

So, yeah, to answer your question, it really doesn't matter if the sun is coming up or if it’s pitch black outside. Morning, noon, or night, if my rear end hits a chair and I’m supposed to just stay put, that restless feeling is going to show up. It’s strictly about the sitting still, regardless of what time the clock says.

It’s just this constant restlessness, and honestly, people are starting to look at me funny during these meetings, so I figured I better come talk to somebody about it.\n\nDoctor: When you get that buzzing, restless feeling, do you also notice your mind racing with thoughts or worries, or is it mainly just your body that feels like it needs to move?\n\nPatient: Well, you know, it’s funny you ask that because my wife always asks me if I’m stressed out or anxious when I’m bouncing my leg or pacing around the living room. But honestly? No, it’s not really my mind racing at all. I mean, I’m not sitting there worrying about paying the electric bill or, uh, whether my truck needs new brake pads—even though it probably does, it's been squeaking a bit when I pull into the lot at work. But anyway, it’s not like that. My head is usually pretty quiet. \n\nIt’s really just... it's all in the body. It’s exactly like my legs have their own separate battery pack that's fully charged, and somebody flipped the switch to 'on', but I’m trying to hold the brakes down. Like at those Tuesday meetings I was telling you about? My brain is actually just trying to listen to my supervisor. He talks a little slow, which maybe doesn't help the situation, but I’m just sitting there thinking about the normal stuff, like the floor buffing schedule for the third-floor hallway. I’m not worried about anything. But my knees are just jumping, and my feet almost feel like they’re resting on hot coals. \n\nIt’s this physical pressure, you know? It builds up right behind my kneecaps, or down in my calves, and if I don't stretch them out or just walk it off, I feel like I’m going to literally jump right out of my own skin. So, yeah, I wouldn't say my thoughts are racing or anything like that. It’s just this overwhelming physical itch to get up and do something, anything, as long as it involves moving my feet.\n\nDoctor: When does this restless feeling tend to happen most? Is it worse at certain times of day, like evenings or nighttime, or does it mainly happen when you're trying to sit still during things like meetings, regardless of what time of day it is?\n\nPatient: You know, I haven't really noticed it being tied to the clock at all. It’s a good question, though, because I actually tried to pay attention to that a while back to see if there was a pattern. \n\nSee, my schedule is pretty regular. I’m usually up by 4:30 in the morning, I clock in by 6:00, and I hit the ground running with the main lobbies and the glass doors because that’s when the foot traffic is lowest. You gotta get the glass done before people start putting their hands all over it, you know? So during those early hours, I’m fine, perfectly fine, because I'm pushing the cart and moving. But then, say it’s around 10:00 AM and I have to sit down at the little desk in the utility closet to fill out the inventory requisition forms for the cleaning chemicals—bam, my legs start going. \n\nBut then again, it happens in the evening, too. I get home, have dinner with my wife, and we like to sit down and watch the evening news or maybe a baseball game if it's on. I’ve got this old recliner, really comfortable, had it for maybe fifteen years. I even reupholstered it myself a few years back. You’d think I’d just melt into it after being on my feet on hard concrete and linoleum for eight hours. But nope. Even at 8:00 PM, if I’m just sitting there watching TV, after about ten or fifteen minutes, that motor starts humming again. I end up having to get up and fiddle with loading the dishwasher, or I'll take the recycling out, or I'll just stand and pace behind the couch while I watch the rest of the game. \n\nSo, yeah, to answer your question, it really doesn't matter if the sun is coming up or if it’s pitch black outside. Morning, noon, or night, if my rear end hits a chair and I’m supposed to just stay put, that restless feeling is going to show up. It’s strictly about the sitting still, regardless of what time the clock says.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, no, not really. I mean, my focus is actually pretty sharp, especially when I'm on the move. Like I was saying about the glass doors in the morning, right? You really have to pay attention to get those streaks out, especially when the morning sun hits them just right. If you miss a spot, everybody sees it, and I take a lot of pride in my work. I've been doing this for a long time, and I know how to get a job done right. 

And finishing things? Oh, I always finish what I start. If I'm stripping and waxing the floors in the main corridor, I can't just stop halfway through because I got distracted. That'd be a disaster! The wax would dry uneven, and then you've got a real mess on your hands. You'd have to strip the whole thing down again, which takes hours, and the floor buffer is heavy, let me tell you. It's an old model, real sturdy, but it'll pull your arms right out of their sockets if you aren't paying attention. So, no, I stay right on task. I get in the zone, honestly. I've got my routine, I know exactly what needs to be done, and I just power through it. 

Even when I'm doing things around the house, like when I reupholstered that recliner I mentioned. That took a lot of concentration. Measuring the fabric, getting the tacks lined up perfectly... I didn't stop until it was done. So I wouldn't say I get distracted or have trouble focusing. It's really just the opposite. I'm focused, I'm getting things done... it's just that I have to be *doing* something. It's only when the doing stops and the sitting starts that I get that itchy, humming feeling in my legs. But as far as my attention goes, I'd say it's completely fine. I don't lose track of things or leave jobs half-finished, not at all.

I don't know, it’s like I’ve got this motor humming right under my skin. Just buzzing away. \n\nLike at work, we have these mandatory staff meetings, usually Tuesday mornings, and we're all crammed in this little break room. The supervisor is going over the supply lists or safety protocols, and I just cannot stay in my seat. I end up shifting my weight, crossing and uncrossing my legs, tapping my boots, and eventually, I just have to stand up and lean against the back wall. Or I’ll find some excuse to move around, like I'll say I need to check the mop buckets or grab another cup of coffee, just to get my legs working. \n\nAnd it’s not just at work, either. I help out a lot at my church, and whenever we have these event briefings—you know, planning the potlucks or the charity drives—we sit in those hard metal folding chairs, and it hits me again. That same humming feeling. I end up getting up to adjust the thermostat or hand out papers just so I don't have to sit there. It’s just this constant restlessness, and honestly, people are starting to look at me funny during these meetings, so I figured I better come talk to somebody about it.\n\nDoctor: When you get that buzzing, restless feeling, do you also notice your mind racing with thoughts or worries, or is it mainly just your body that feels like it needs to move?\n\nPatient: Well, you know, it’s funny you ask that because my wife always asks me if I’m stressed out or anxious when I’m bouncing my leg or pacing around the living room. But honestly? No, it’s not really my mind racing at all. I mean, I’m not sitting there worrying about paying the electric bill or, uh, whether my truck needs new brake pads—even though it probably does, it's been squeaking a bit when I pull into the lot at work. But anyway, it’s not like that. My head is usually pretty quiet. \n\nIt’s really just... it's all in the body. It’s exactly like my legs have their own separate battery pack that's fully charged, and somebody flipped the switch to 'on', but I’m trying to hold the brakes down. Like at those Tuesday meetings I was telling you about? My brain is actually just trying to listen to my supervisor. He talks a little slow, which maybe doesn't help the situation, but I’m just sitting there thinking about the normal stuff, like the floor buffing schedule for the third-floor hallway. I’m not worried about anything. But my knees are just jumping, and my feet almost feel like they’re resting on hot coals. \n\nIt’s this physical pressure, you know? It builds up right behind my kneecaps, or down in my calves, and if I don't stretch them out or just walk it off, I feel like I’m going to literally jump right out of my own skin. So, yeah, I wouldn't say my thoughts are racing or anything like that. It’s just this overwhelming physical itch to get up and do something, anything, as long as it involves moving my feet.\n\nDoctor: When does this restless feeling tend to happen most? Is it worse at certain times of day, like evenings or nighttime, or does it mainly happen when you're trying to sit still during things like meetings, regardless of what time of day it is?\n\nPatient: You know, I haven't really noticed it being tied to the clock at all. It’s a good question, though, because I actually tried to pay attention to that a while back to see if there was a pattern. \n\nSee, my schedule is pretty regular. I’m usually up by 4:30 in the morning, I clock in by 6:00, and I hit the ground running with the main lobbies and the glass doors because that’s when the foot traffic is lowest. You gotta get the glass done before people start putting their hands all over it, you know? So during those early hours, I’m fine, perfectly fine, because I'm pushing the cart and moving. But then, say it’s around 10:00 AM and I have to sit down at the little desk in the utility closet to fill out the inventory requisition forms for the cleaning chemicals—bam, my legs start going. \n\nBut then again, it happens in the evening, too. I get home, have dinner with my wife, and we like to sit down and watch the evening news or maybe a baseball game if it's on. I’ve got this old recliner, really comfortable, had it for maybe fifteen years. I even reupholstered it myself a few years back. You’d think I’d just melt into it after being on my feet on hard concrete and linoleum for eight hours. But nope. Even at 8:00 PM, if I’m just sitting there watching TV, after about ten or fifteen minutes, that motor starts humming again. I end up having to get up and fiddle with loading the dishwasher, or I'll take the recycling out, or I'll just stand and pace behind the couch while I watch the rest of the game. \n\nSo, yeah, to answer your question, it really doesn't matter if the sun is coming up or if it’s pitch black outside. Morning, noon, or night, if my rear end hits a chair and I’m supposed to just stay put, that restless feeling is going to show up. It’s strictly about the sitting still, regardless of what time the clock says.\n\nDoctor: Besides the restlessness when you're sitting, do you find it hard to focus or pay attention to tasks—like, do you get distracted easily, or do you struggle to finish things you start, even when you're moving around and working?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. I mean, my focus is actually pretty sharp, especially when I'm on the move. Like I was saying about the glass doors in the morning, right? You really have to pay attention to get those streaks out, especially when the morning sun hits them just right. If you miss a spot, everybody sees it, and I take a lot of pride in my work. I've been doing this for a long time, and I know how to get a job done right. \n\nAnd finishing things? Oh, I always finish what I start. If I'm stripping and waxing the floors in the main corridor, I can't just stop halfway through because I got distracted. That'd be a disaster! The wax would dry uneven, and then you've got a real mess on your hands. You'd have to strip the whole thing down again, which takes hours, and the floor buffer is heavy, let me tell you. It's an old model, real sturdy, but it'll pull your arms right out of their sockets if you aren't paying attention. So, no, I stay right on task. I get in the zone, honestly. I've got my routine, I know exactly what needs to be done, and I just power through it. \n\nEven when I'm doing things around the house, like when I reupholstered that recliner I mentioned. It's really just the opposite. I'm focused, I'm getting things done... it's just that I have to be *doing* something. It's only when the doing stops and the sitting starts that I get that itchy, humming feeling in my legs. But as far as my attention goes, I'd say it's completely fine. I don't lose track of things or leave jobs half-finished, not at all.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. It's an old model, real sturdy, but it'll pull your arms right out of their sockets if you aren't paying attention. So, no, I stay right on task. I get in the zone, honestly. I've got my routine, I know exactly what needs to be done, and I just power through it. \n\nEven when I'm doing things around the house, like when I reupholstered that recliner I mentioned. That took a lot of concentration. Measuring the fabric, getting the tacks lined up perfectly... I didn't stop until it was done. So I wouldn't say I get distracted or have trouble focusing. It's really just the opposite. I'm focused, I'm getting things done... it's just that I have to be *doing* something. It's only when the doing stops and the sitting starts that I get that itchy, humming feeling in my legs. But as far as my attention goes, I'd say it's completely fine. I don't lose track of things or leave jobs half-finished, not at all.\n\n[Last Question]\nWhen you get that buzzing, restless feeling in your legs, do you feel like you absolutely have to move them, or could you sit still if you really needed to—like if you were at a doctor's office and the doctor asked you to stay in your seat for an exam?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },

It’s exactly like my legs have their own separate battery pack that's fully charged, and somebody flipped the switch to 'on', but I’m trying to hold the brakes down. I’m not worried about anything. But my knees are just jumping, and my feet almost feel like they’re resting on hot coals. \n\nIt’s this physical pressure, you know? It builds up right behind my kneecaps, or down in my calves, and if I don't stretch them out or just walk it off, I feel like I’m going to literally jump right out of my own skin. It’s just this overwhelming physical itch to get up and do something, anything, as long as it involves moving my feet.\n\nDoctor: When does this restless feeling tend to happen most? Is it worse at certain times of day, like evenings or nighttime, or does it mainly happen when you're trying to sit still during things like meetings, regardless of what time of day it is?\n\nPatient: You know, I haven't really noticed it being tied to the clock at all. It’s a good question, though, because I actually tried to pay attention to that a while back to see if there was a pattern. \n\nSee, my schedule is pretty regular. I’m usually up by 4:30 in the morning, I clock in by 6:00, and I hit the ground running with the main lobbies and the glass doors because that’s when the foot traffic is lowest. You gotta get the glass done before people start putting their hands all over it, you know? So during those early hours, I’m fine, perfectly fine, because I'm pushing the cart and moving. But then, say it’s around 10:00 AM and I have to sit down at the little desk in the utility closet to fill out the inventory requisition forms for the cleaning chemicals—bam, my legs start going. \n\nBut then again, it happens in the evening, too. I get home, have dinner with my wife, and we like to sit down and watch the evening news or maybe a baseball game if it's on. I’ve got this old recliner, really comfortable, had it for maybe fifteen years. I even reupholstered it myself a few years back. You’d think I’d just melt into it after being on my feet on hard concrete and linoleum for eight hours. But nope. Even at 8:00 PM, if I’m just sitting there watching TV, after about ten or fifteen minutes, that motor starts humming again. I end up having to get up and fiddle with loading the dishwasher, or I'll take the recycling out, or I'll just stand and pace behind the couch while I watch the rest of the game. \n\nSo, yeah, to answer your question, it really doesn't matter if the sun is coming up or if it’s pitch black outside. Morning, noon, or night, if my rear end hits a chair and I’m supposed to just stay put, that restless feeling is going to show up. It’s strictly about the sitting still, regardless of what time the clock says.\n\nDoctor: Besides the restlessness when you're sitting, do you find it hard to focus or pay attention to tasks—like, do you get distracted easily, or do you struggle to finish things you start, even when you're moving around and working?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. I mean, my focus is actually pretty sharp, especially when I'm on the move. Like I was saying about the glass doors in the morning, right? You really have to pay attention to get those streaks out, especially when the morning sun hits them just right. If you miss a spot, everybody sees it, and I take a lot of pride in my work. I've been doing this for a long time, and I know how to get a job done right. \n\nAnd finishing things? Oh, I always finish what I start. If I'm stripping and waxing the floors in the main corridor, I can't just stop halfway through because I got distracted. That'd be a disaster! The wax would dry uneven, and then you've got a real mess on your hands. You'd have to strip the whole thing down again, which takes hours, and the floor buffer is heavy, let me tell you. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, see, that's the tricky part. I mean... I guess I try? But honestly, I don't think I can. Not completely. 

It’s funny you use a doctor's office as an example, because I was actually just at the dentist a couple of weeks ago for my regular cleaning. I go to Dr. Henderson over on Elm Street—nice guy, I've been going to him for years, but they are always running behind schedule. Anyway, you know how it is, you're leaning back in that chair, they've got the little paper bib clipped around your neck, and the hygienist—her name is Sarah, she's great, always remembers to ask about my wife's garden—she's in there with that sharp little metal scraper. So obviously, I know I can't be jumping around, right? It's just common sense, you don't move when someone's poking around your gums. 

So I tell myself, "Just stay put." And I do, for maybe a minute or two. I grip the plastic armrests, I stare up at that fluorescent light on the ceiling—which, by the way, was flickering the whole time. As a maintenance guy, that was driving me absolutely crazy. I know exactly what kind of fixture it is, and I almost offered to just go out to my truck and grab a new ballast to fix it for them right then and there. But anyway, I’m laying there, trying to stay completely still. 

But that buzzing feeling in my legs? It doesn't care that I'm at the dentist. It doesn't care about the metal scraper. It just keeps building and building. It's exactly like... well, it's like if you shake up a can of soda and then try to hold your thumb over the top to keep it from exploding. The pressure doesn't just go away because you want it to. So even though I'm trying my absolute hardest not to move my body, I end up having to do *something*. I'll start wiggling my toes furiously inside my work boots, or I'll be clenching and unclenching my calf muscles over and over, or I'll just start shifting my hips back and forth against the vinyl of the chair. 

And even doing all that, it's just agonizing. It takes every single ounce of my willpower just to keep my knees from bouncing right up into the tray of dental tools. So, to answer your question... could I sit perfectly still like a statue if I really, really had to? No, I really don't think I could. I absolutely have to let that energy out somehow, even if it's just tapping my heel against the bottom of the chair, otherwise I feel like I'm going to literally jump out of my skin.

You’d think I’d just melt into it after being on my feet on hard concrete and linoleum for eight hours. But nope. Even at 8:00 PM, if I’m just sitting there watching TV, after about ten or fifteen minutes, that motor starts humming again. I end up having to get up and fiddle with loading the dishwasher, or I'll take the recycling out, or I'll just stand and pace behind the couch while I watch the rest of the game. \n\nSo, yeah, to answer your question, it really doesn't matter if the sun is coming up or if it’s pitch black outside. Morning, noon, or night, if my rear end hits a chair and I’m supposed to just stay put, that restless feeling is going to show up. It’s strictly about the sitting still, regardless of what time the clock says.\n\nDoctor: Besides the restlessness when you're sitting, do you find it hard to focus or pay attention to tasks—like, do you get distracted easily, or do you struggle to finish things you start, even when you're moving around and working?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. I mean, my focus is actually pretty sharp, especially when I'm on the move. Like I was saying about the glass doors in the morning, right? You really have to pay attention to get those streaks out, especially when the morning sun hits them just right. If you miss a spot, everybody sees it, and I take a lot of pride in my work. I've been doing this for a long time, and I know how to get a job done right. \n\nAnd finishing things? Oh, I always finish what I start. If I'm stripping and waxing the floors in the main corridor, I can't just stop halfway through because I got distracted. That'd be a disaster! The wax would dry uneven, and then you've got a real mess on your hands. You'd have to strip the whole thing down again, which takes hours, and the floor buffer is heavy, let me tell you. It's an old model, real sturdy, but it'll pull your arms right out of their sockets if you aren't paying attention. So, no, I stay right on task. I get in the zone, honestly. I've got my routine, I know exactly what needs to be done, and I just power through it. \n\nEven when I'm doing things around the house, like when I reupholstered that recliner I mentioned. That took a lot of concentration. Measuring the fabric, getting the tacks lined up perfectly... I didn't stop until it was done. So I wouldn't say I get distracted or have trouble focusing. It's really just the opposite. I'm focused, I'm getting things done... it's just that I have to be *doing* something. It's only when the doing stops and the sitting starts that I get that itchy, humming feeling in my legs. But as far as my attention goes, I'd say it's completely fine. I don't lose track of things or leave jobs half-finished, not at all.\n\nDoctor: When you get that buzzing, restless feeling in your legs, do you feel like you absolutely have to move them, or could you sit still if you really needed to—like if you were at a doctor's office and the doctor asked you to stay in your seat for an exam?\n\nPatient: Well, see, that's the tricky part. I mean... I guess I try? But honestly, I don't think I can. Not completely. \n\nIt’s funny you use a doctor's office as an example, because I was actually just at the dentist a couple of weeks ago for my regular cleaning. I go to Dr. Henderson over on Elm Street—nice guy, I've been going to him for years, but they are always running behind schedule. Anyway, you know how it is, you're leaning back in that chair, they've got the little paper bib clipped around your neck, and the hygienist—her name is Sarah, she's great, always remembers to ask about my wife's garden—she's in there with that sharp little metal scraper. So obviously, I know I can't be jumping around, right? It's just common sense, you don't move when someone's poking around your gums. \n\nSo I tell myself, \"Just stay put.\" And I do, for maybe a minute or two. I grip the plastic armrests, I stare up at that fluorescent light on the ceiling—which, by the way, was flickering the whole time. As a maintenance guy, that was driving me absolutely crazy. I know exactly what kind of fixture it is, and I almost offered to just go out to my truck and grab a new ballast to fix it for them right then and there. But anyway, I’m laying there, trying to stay completely still. \n\nBut that buzzing feeling in my legs? It doesn't care that I'm at the dentist. It doesn't care about the metal scraper. It just keeps building and building. It's exactly like... well, it's like if you shake up a can of soda and then try to hold your thumb over the top to keep it from exploding. The pressure doesn't just go away because you want it to. So even though I'm trying my absolute hardest not to move my body, I end up having to do *something*. I'll start wiggling my toes furiously inside my work boots, or I'll be clenching and unclenching my calf muscles over and over, or I'll just start shifting my hips back and forth against the vinyl of the chair. \n\nAnd even doing all that, it's just agonizing. It takes every single ounce of my willpower just to keep my knees from bouncing right up into the tray of dental tools. So, to answer your question... could I sit perfectly still like a statue if I really, really had to? No, I really don't think I could. I absolutely have to let that energy out somehow, even if it's just tapping my heel against the bottom of the chair, otherwise I feel like I'm going to literally jump out of my skin.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Pain? No, no, it’s definitely not pain. I mean, I know what pain feels like, believe me. You spend thirty-something years walking on polished concrete and terrazzo floors, pushing heavy equipment around, and your joints are absolutely going to let you know about it. I’ve got a bit of normal wear and tear, maybe a little arthritis in my left knee—flares up when it rains, or when the barometer drops, you know how that goes. That’s a real ache. That’s a sharp, physical pain where you want to take an ibuprofen and put a bag of frozen peas on it. 

But this buzzing thing? It’s completely different. It doesn't hurt at all. And it’s not a tingle, either. It’s not like when you sit on your foot wrong and it falls asleep, and you get those horrible pins and needles when the blood starts rushing back. It’s nothing like that. No burning, no numbness, nothing that would make you think a nerve is pinched or something. 

It really is just... exactly what you said a second ago. An urge. A drive. It’s like... well, it’s like my legs just woke up and decided it’s time to run a marathon, even if the rest of my brain is just trying to watch the local weather report. There’s no hurt to it, it’s just this intense, coiled-up energy. 

Think of it like a wind-up toy, you know? You twist that little metal key on the side until it won’t turn anymore, and you can feel the tension in the spring just waiting to be let go. If you hold the wheels so they can't spin, it doesn't *hurt* the toy, but the whole thing is just vibrating in your hand, right? It's just fighting you, wanting to spin. That’s my legs. They just want to go. They *need* to go. And if I don’t let them, if I force them to stay still like in that dentist chair I was telling you about, that’s when it gets frustrating. Not painful, just... maddening, really. It's this overwhelming physical demand to just move, and you just can't ignore it, no matter how hard you try to tune it out. So yeah, no pain, just... a motor that won't shut off.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n6\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nG25.81\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, and welcome. I'm Dr. [Name], a psychiatrist. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, hi, Doctor. Thanks for fitting me in. I, uh, I guess what brings me in today is... well, it’s this feeling I get, and it's been driving me a bit crazy lately. \n\nSee, I work as a building cleaner—janitorial stuff, maintenance, keeping the place running. And when I’m on the clock doing my rounds, I’m okay, because I’m always moving. But the absolute second I’m supposed to just sit still, it’s like... I don't know, it’s like I’ve got this motor humming right under my skin. Just buzzing away. \n\nLike at work, we have these mandatory staff meetings, usually Tuesday mornings, and we're all crammed in this little break room. The supervisor is going over the supply lists or safety protocols, and I just cannot stay in my seat. I end up shifting my weight, crossing and uncrossing my legs, tapping my boots, and eventually, I just have to stand up and lean against the back wall. Or I’ll find some excuse to move around, like I'll say I need to check the mop buckets or grab another cup of coffee, just to get my legs working. \n\nAnd it’s not just at work, either. I help out a lot at my church, and whenever we have these event briefings—you know, planning the potlucks or the charity drives—we sit in those hard metal folding chairs, and it hits me again. That same humming feeling. I end up getting up to adjust the thermostat or hand out papers just so I don't have to sit there. It’s just this constant restlessness, and honestly, people are starting to look at me funny during these meetings, so I figured I better come talk to somebody about it.\n\nDoctor: When you get that buzzing, restless feeling, do you also notice your mind racing with thoughts or worries, or is it mainly just your body that feels like it needs to move?\n\nPatient: Well, you know, it’s funny you ask that because my wife always asks me if I’m stressed out or anxious when I’m bouncing my leg or pacing around the living room. But honestly? No, it’s not really my mind racing at all. I mean, I’m not sitting there worrying about paying the electric bill or, uh, whether my truck needs new brake pads—even though it probably does, it's been squeaking a bit when I pull into the lot at work. But anyway, it’s not like that. My head is usually pretty quiet. \n\nIt’s really just... it's all in the body. It’s exactly like my legs have their own separate battery pack that's fully charged, and somebody flipped the switch to 'on', but I’m trying to hold the brakes down. Like at those Tuesday meetings I was telling you about? My brain is actually just trying to listen to my supervisor. He talks a little slow, which maybe doesn't help the situation, but I’m just sitting there thinking about the normal stuff, like the floor buffing schedule for the third-floor hallway. I’m not worried about anything. But my knees are just jumping, and my feet almost feel like they’re resting on hot coals. \n\nIt’s this physical pressure, you know? It builds up right behind my kneecaps, or down in my calves, and if I don't stretch them out or just walk it off, I feel like I’m going to literally jump right out of my own skin. So, yeah, I wouldn't say my thoughts are racing or anything like that. It’s just this overwhelming physical itch to get up and do something, anything, as long as it involves moving my feet.\n\nDoctor: When does this restless feeling tend to happen most? Is it worse at certain times of day, like evenings or nighttime, or does it mainly happen when you're trying to sit still during things like meetings, regardless of what time of day it is?\n\nPatient: You know, I haven't really noticed it being tied to the clock at all. It’s a good question, though, because I actually tried to pay attention to that a while back to see if there was a pattern. \n\nSee, my schedule is pretty regular. I’m usually up by 4:30 in the morning, I clock in by 6:00, and I hit the ground running with the main lobbies and the glass doors because that’s when the foot traffic is lowest. You gotta get the glass done before people start putting their hands all over it, you know? So during those early hours, I’m fine, perfectly fine, because I'm pushing the cart and moving. But then, say it’s around 10:00 AM and I have to sit down at the little desk in the utility closet to fill out the inventory requisition forms for the cleaning chemicals—bam, my legs start going. \n\nBut then again, it happens in the evening, too. I get home, have dinner with my wife, and we like to sit down and watch the evening news or maybe a baseball game if it's on. I’ve got this old recliner, really comfortable, had it for maybe fifteen years. I even reupholstered it myself a few years back. You’d think I’d just melt into it after being on my feet on hard concrete and linoleum for eight hours. But nope. Even at 8:00 PM, if I’m just sitting there watching TV, after about ten or fifteen minutes, that motor starts humming again. I end up having to get up and fiddle with loading the dishwasher, or I'll take the recycling out, or I'll just stand and pace behind the couch while I watch the rest of the game. \n\nSo, yeah, to answer your question, it really doesn't matter if the sun is coming up or if it’s pitch black outside. Morning, noon, or night, if my rear end hits a chair and I’m supposed to just stay put, that restless feeling is going to show up. It’s strictly about the sitting still, regardless of what time the clock says.\n\nDoctor: Besides the restlessness when you're sitting, do you find it hard to focus or pay attention to tasks—like, do you get distracted easily, or do you struggle to finish things you start, even when you're moving around and working?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. I mean, my focus is actually pretty sharp, especially when I'm on the move. Like I was saying about the glass doors in the morning, right? You really have to pay attention to get those streaks out, especially when the morning sun hits them just right. If you miss a spot, everybody sees it, and I take a lot of pride in my work. I've been doing this for a long time, and I know how to get a job done right. \n\nAnd finishing things? Oh, I always finish what I start. If I'm stripping and waxing the floors in the main corridor, I can't just stop halfway through because I got distracted. That'd be a disaster! The wax would dry uneven, and then you've got a real mess on your hands. You'd have to strip the whole thing down again, which takes hours, and the floor buffer is heavy, let me tell you. It's an old model, real sturdy, but it'll pull your arms right out of their sockets if you aren't paying attention. So, no, I stay right on task. I get in the zone, honestly. I've got my routine, I know exactly what needs to be done, and I just power through it. \n\nEven when I'm doing things around the house, like when I reupholstered that recliner I mentioned. That took a lot of concentration. Measuring the fabric, getting the tacks lined up perfectly... I didn't stop until it was done. So I wouldn't say I get distracted or have trouble focusing. It's really just the opposite. I'm focused, I'm getting things done... it's just that I have to be *doing* something. It's only when the doing stops and the sitting starts that I get that itchy, humming feeling in my legs. But as far as my attention goes, I'd say it's completely fine. I don't lose track of things or leave jobs half-finished, not at all.\n\nDoctor: When you get that buzzing, restless feeling in your legs, do you feel like you absolutely have to move them, or could you sit still if you really needed to—like if you were at a doctor's office and the doctor asked you to stay in your seat for an exam?\n\nPatient: Well, see, that's the tricky part. I mean... I guess I try? But honestly, I don't think I can. Not completely. \n\nIt’s funny you use a doctor's office as an example, because I was actually just at the dentist a couple of weeks ago for my regular cleaning. I go to Dr. Henderson over on Elm Street—nice guy, I've been going to him for years, but they are always running behind schedule. Anyway, you know how it is, you're leaning back in that chair, they've got the little paper bib clipped around your neck, and the hygienist—her name is Sarah, she's great, always remembers to ask about my wife's garden—she's in there with that sharp little metal scraper. So obviously, I know I can't be jumping around, right? It's just common sense, you don't move when someone's poking around your gums. \n\nSo I tell myself, \"Just stay put.\" And I do, for maybe a minute or two. I grip the plastic armrests, I stare up at that fluorescent light on the ceiling—which, by the way, was flickering the whole time. As a maintenance guy, that was driving me absolutely crazy. I know exactly what kind of fixture it is, and I almost offered to just go out to my truck and grab a new ballast to fix it for them right then and there. But anyway, I’m laying there, trying to stay completely still. \n\nBut that buzzing feeling in my legs? It doesn't care that I'm at the dentist. It doesn't care about the metal scraper. It just keeps building and building. It's exactly like... well, it's like if you shake up a can of soda and then try to hold your thumb over the top to keep it from exploding. The pressure doesn't just go away because you want it to. So even though I'm trying my absolute hardest not to move my body, I end up having to do *something*. I'll start wiggling my toes furiously inside my work boots, or I'll be clenching and unclenching my calf muscles over and over, or I'll just start shifting my hips back and forth against the vinyl of the chair. \n\nAnd even doing all that, it's just agonizing. It takes every single ounce of my willpower just to keep my knees from bouncing right up into the tray of dental tools. So, to answer your question... could I sit perfectly still like a statue if I really, really had to? No, I really don't think I could. I absolutely have to let that energy out somehow, even if it's just tapping my heel against the bottom of the chair, otherwise I feel like I'm going to literally jump out of my skin.\n\nDoctor: When you describe that buzzing feeling and the pressure behind your kneecaps, can you tell me more about what that sensation actually feels like? Is it uncomfortable or painful, like tingling or burning? Or is it more just an urge or drive to move without any particular uncomfortable feeling?\n\nPatient: Pain? No, no, it’s definitely not pain. I mean, I know what pain feels like, believe me. You spend thirty-something years walking on polished concrete and terrazzo floors, pushing heavy equipment around, and your joints are absolutely going to let you know about it. I’ve got a bit of normal wear and tear, maybe a little arthritis in my left knee—flares up when it rains, or when the barometer drops, you know how that goes. That’s a real ache. That’s a sharp, physical pain where you want to take an ibuprofen and put a bag of frozen peas on it. \n\nBut this buzzing thing? It’s completely different. It doesn't hurt at all. And it’s not a tingle, either. It’s not like when you sit on your foot wrong and it falls asleep, and you get those horrible pins and needles when the blood starts rushing back. It’s nothing like that. No burning, no numbness, nothing that would make you think a nerve is pinched or something. \n\nIt really is just... exactly what you said a second ago. An urge. A drive. It’s like... well, it’s like my legs just woke up and decided it’s time to run a marathon, even if the rest of my brain is just trying to watch the local weather report. There’s no hurt to it, it’s just this intense, coiled-up energy. \n\nThink of it like a wind-up toy, you know? You twist that little metal key on the side until it won’t turn anymore, and you can feel the tension in the spring just waiting to be let go. If you hold the wheels so they can't spin, it doesn't *hurt* the toy, but the whole thing is just vibrating in your hand, right? It's just fighting you, wanting to spin. That’s my legs. They just want to go. They *need* to go. And if I don’t let them, if I force them to stay still like in that dentist chair I was telling you about, that’s when it gets frustrating. Not painful, just... maddening, really. It's this overwhelming physical demand to just move, and you just can't ignore it, no matter how hard you try to tune it out. So yeah, no pain, just... a motor that won't shut off.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a patient who has come to a psychiatric clinic with Attention-Deficit/Hyperactivity Disorder (Predominantly Hyperactive/Impulsive Presentation). I mean, my focus is actually pretty sharp, especially when I'm on the move. Like I was saying about the glass doors in the morning, right? You really have to pay attention to get those streaks out, especially when the morning sun hits them just right. If you miss a spot, everybody sees it, and I take a lot of pride in my work. I've been doing this for a long time, and I know how to get a job done right. \n\nAnd finishing things? Oh, I always finish what I start. If I'm stripping and waxing the floors in the main corridor, I can't just stop halfway through because I got distracted. That'd be a disaster! The wax would dry uneven, and then you've got a real mess on your hands. You'd have to strip the whole thing down again, which takes hours, and the floor buffer is heavy, let me tell you. It's an old model, real sturdy, but it'll pull your arms right out of their sockets if you aren't paying attention. So, no, I stay right on task. I get in the zone, honestly. I've got my routine, I know exactly what needs to be done, and I just power through it. \n\nEven when I'm doing things around the house, like when I reupholstered that recliner I mentioned. That took a lot of concentration. Measuring the fabric, getting the tacks lined up perfectly... I didn't stop until it was done. So I wouldn't say I get distracted or have trouble focusing. It's really just the opposite. I'm focused, I'm getting things done... it's just that I have to be *doing* something. It's only when the doing stops and the sitting starts that I get that itchy, humming feeling in my legs. But as far as my attention goes, I'd say it's completely fine. I don't lose track of things or leave jobs half-finished, not at all.\n\nDoctor: When you get that buzzing, restless feeling in your legs, do you feel like you absolutely have to move them, or could you sit still if you really needed to—like if you were at a doctor's office and the doctor asked you to stay in your seat for an exam?\n\nPatient: Well, see, that's the tricky part. I mean... I guess I try? But honestly, I don't think I can. Not completely. \n\nIt’s funny you use a doctor's office as an example, because I was actually just at the dentist a couple of weeks ago for my regular cleaning. I go to Dr. Henderson over on Elm Street—nice guy, I've been going to him for years, but they are always running behind schedule. Anyway, you know how it is, you're leaning back in that chair, they've got the little paper bib clipped around your neck, and the hygienist—her name is Sarah, she's great, always remembers to ask about my wife's garden—she's in there with that sharp little metal scraper. So obviously, I know I can't be jumping around, right? It's just common sense, you don't move when someone's poking around your gums. \n\nSo I tell myself, \"Just stay put.\" And I do, for maybe a minute or two. I grip the plastic armrests, I stare up at that fluorescent light on the ceiling—which, by the way, was flickering the whole time. As a maintenance guy, that was driving me absolutely crazy. I know exactly what kind of fixture it is, and I almost offered to just go out to my truck and grab a new ballast to fix it for them right then and there. But anyway, I’m laying there, trying to stay completely still. \n\nBut that buzzing feeling in my legs? It doesn't care that I'm at the dentist. It doesn't care about the metal scraper. It just keeps building and building. It's exactly like... well, it's like if you shake up a can of soda and then try to hold your thumb over the top to keep it from exploding. The pressure doesn't just go away because you want it to. So even though I'm trying my absolute hardest not to move my body, I end up having to do *something*. I'll start wiggling my toes furiously inside my work boots, or I'll be clenching and unclenching my calf muscles over and over, or I'll just start shifting my hips back and forth against the vinyl of the chair. \n\nAnd even doing all that, it's just agonizing. It takes every single ounce of my willpower just to keep my knees from bouncing right up into the tray of dental tools. I’ve got a bit of normal wear and tear, maybe a little arthritis in my left knee—flares up when it rains, or when the barometer drops, you know how that goes. That’s a real ache. That’s a sharp, physical pain where you want to take an ibuprofen and put a bag of frozen peas on it. \n\nBut this buzzing thing? It’s completely different. It doesn't hurt at all. And it’s not a tingle, either. It’s not like when you sit on your foot wrong and it falls asleep, and you get those horrible pins and needles when the blood starts rushing back. It’s nothing like that. No burning, no numbness, nothing that would make you think a nerve is pinched or something. \n\nIt really is just... exactly what you said a second ago. An urge. A drive. It’s like... well, it’s like my legs just woke up and decided it’s time to run a marathon, even if the rest of my brain is just trying to watch the local weather report. There’s no hurt to it, it’s just this intense, coiled-up energy. \n\nThink of it like a wind-up toy, you know? You twist that little metal key on the side until it won’t turn anymore, and you can feel the tension in the spring just waiting to be let go. If you hold the wheels so they can't spin, it doesn't *hurt* the toy, but the whole thing is just vibrating in your hand, right? It's just fighting you, wanting to spin. That’s my legs. They just want to go. They *need* to go. And if I don’t let them, if I force them to stay still like in that dentist chair I was telling you about, that’s when it gets frustrating. Not painful, just... maddening, really. It's this overwhelming physical demand to just move, and you just can't ignore it, no matter how hard you try to tune it out. So yeah, no pain, just... a motor that won't shut off.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever tried taking any medications—prescription or over-the-counter—to help with this restless feeling in your legs, and if so, did any of them actually help?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. Well, no, I haven’t. I mean, it never really crossed my mind to take a pill for something like this, to be completely honest with you. Like I said before, I’ll take an ibuprofen or maybe an Aleve if my knee is really acting up after a long shift of buffing the hallways, because that’s an actual ache, right? You go to the pharmacy, you see a hundred different bottles for pain or headaches or allergies. But I’ve never exactly seen a box on the shelf at Walgreens that says, you know, "for when your legs feel like a wind-up toy."

I just always figured it was just... me. Just how I’m wired. Too much energy, or a motor running hot. How do you even medicate that anyway? What kind of pill just stops your legs from wanting to move without knocking you out completely? I can't be taking sleeping pills or something that makes me drowsy, I've got a whole building to maintain! If a pipe bursts in the second-floor men's room at 2 PM, I can't be groggy. I have to be sharp, ready to grab the wrench and get to work. 

So, no, prescription, over-the-counter, vitamins, whatever... I haven't tried any of it for the restlessness. My wife, she’s always trying to get me to drink chamomile tea or cut back on my coffee, thinking maybe it's the caffeine making me jittery. But I've been drinking the exact same thermos of Folgers dark roast every single morning since about 1992, and let me tell you, it doesn't make a lick of difference whether I have one cup or four. The buzzing is just there the second I sit down, coffee or no coffee. 

So I guess my only "medicine" for it is just... getting up and doing something. Grabbing a broom, pacing the room, doing a lap around the gymnasium to check the bleachers. That's the only thing that actually helps. But as far as actual medication goes? No, never tried anything. Never even asked a doctor about it before today, honestly. I just didn't think there was anything in a bottle that could fix it.

It’s a good question, though, because I actually tried to pay attention to that a while back to see if there was a pattern. \n\nSee, my schedule is pretty regular. I’m usually up by 4:30 in the morning, I clock in by 6:00, and I hit the ground running with the main lobbies and the glass doors because that’s when the foot traffic is lowest. You’d think I’d just melt into it after being on my feet on hard concrete and linoleum for eight hours. But nope. Even at 8:00 PM, if I’m just sitting there watching TV, after about ten or fifteen minutes, that motor starts humming again. I end up having to get up and fiddle with loading the dishwasher, or I'll take the recycling out, or I'll just stand and pace behind the couch while I watch the rest of the game. \n\nSo, yeah, to answer your question, it really doesn't matter if the sun is coming up or if it’s pitch black outside. It’s strictly about the sitting still, regardless of what time the clock says.\n\nDoctor: Besides the restlessness when you're sitting, do you find it hard to focus or pay attention to tasks—like, do you get distracted easily, or do you struggle to finish things you start, even when you're moving around and working?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. The wax would dry uneven, and then you've got a real mess on your hands. You'd have to strip the whole thing down again, which takes hours, and the floor buffer is heavy, let me tell you. I've got my routine, I know exactly what needs to be done, and I just power through it. \n\nEven when I'm doing things around the house, like when I reupholstered that recliner I mentioned. That took a lot of concentration. Measuring the fabric, getting the tacks lined up perfectly... I didn't stop until it was done. So I wouldn't say I get distracted or have trouble focusing. It's really just the opposite. I'm focused, I'm getting things done... it's just that I have to be *doing* something. It's only when the doing stops and the sitting starts that I get that itchy, humming feeling in my legs. But as far as my attention goes, I'd say it's completely fine. I don't lose track of things or leave jobs half-finished, not at all.\n\nDoctor: When you get that buzzing, restless feeling in your legs, do you feel like you absolutely have to move them, or could you sit still if you really needed to—like if you were at a doctor's office and the doctor asked you to stay in your seat for an exam?\n\nPatient: Well, see, that's the tricky part. I mean... I guess I try? But honestly, I don't think I can. Not completely. \n\nIt’s funny you use a doctor's office as an example, because I was actually just at the dentist a couple of weeks ago for my regular cleaning. I go to Dr. Henderson over on Elm Street—nice guy, I've been going to him for years, but they are always running behind schedule. Anyway, you know how it is, you're leaning back in that chair, they've got the little paper bib clipped around your neck, and the hygienist—her name is Sarah, she's great, always remembers to ask about my wife's garden—she's in there with that sharp little metal scraper. So obviously, I know I can't be jumping around, right? It's just common sense, you don't move when someone's poking around your gums. \n\nSo I tell myself, \"Just stay put.\" And I do, for maybe a minute or two. I grip the plastic armrests, I stare up at that fluorescent light on the ceiling—which, by the way, was flickering the whole time. As a maintenance guy, that was driving me absolutely crazy. I know exactly what kind of fixture it is, and I almost offered to just go out to my truck and grab a new ballast to fix it for them right then and there. But anyway, I’m laying there, trying to stay completely still. \n\nBut that buzzing feeling in my legs? It doesn't care that I'm at the dentist. It doesn't care about the metal scraper. It just keeps building and building. It's exactly like... well, it's like if you shake up a can of soda and then try to hold your thumb over the top to keep it from exploding. The pressure doesn't just go away because you want it to. So even though I'm trying my absolute hardest not to move my body, I end up having to do *something*. I'll start wiggling my toes furiously inside my work boots, or I'll be clenching and unclenching my calf muscles over and over, or I'll just start shifting my hips back and forth against the vinyl of the chair. \n\nAnd even doing all that, it's just agonizing. It takes every single ounce of my willpower just to keep my knees from bouncing right up into the tray of dental tools. So, to answer your question... could I sit perfectly still like a statue if I really, really had to? No, I really don't think I could. I absolutely have to let that energy out somehow, even if it's just tapping my heel against the bottom of the chair, otherwise I feel like I'm going to literally jump out of my skin.\n\nDoctor: When you describe that buzzing feeling and the pressure behind your kneecaps, can you tell me more about what that sensation actually feels like? Is it uncomfortable or painful, like tingling or burning? Or is it more just an urge or drive to move without any particular uncomfortable feeling?\n\nPatient: Pain? No, no, it’s definitely not pain. I mean, I know what pain feels like, believe me. You spend thirty-something years walking on polished concrete and terrazzo floors, pushing heavy equipment around, and your joints are absolutely going to let you know about it. I’ve got a bit of normal wear and tear, maybe a little arthritis in my left knee—flares up when it rains, or when the barometer drops, you know how that goes. That’s a real ache. That’s a sharp, physical pain where you want to take an ibuprofen and put a bag of frozen peas on it. \n\nBut this buzzing thing? It’s completely different. It doesn't hurt at all. And it’s not a tingle, either. It’s not like when you sit on your foot wrong and it falls asleep, and you get those horrible pins and needles when the blood starts rushing back. It’s nothing like that. No burning, no numbness, nothing that would make you think a nerve is pinched or something. \n\nIt really is just... exactly what you said a second ago. An urge. A drive. It’s like... well, it’s like my legs just woke up and decided it’s time to run a marathon, even if the rest of my brain is just trying to watch the local weather report. There’s no hurt to it, it’s just this intense, coiled-up energy. \n\nThink of it like a wind-up toy, you know? You twist that little metal key on the side until it won’t turn anymore, and you can feel the tension in the spring just waiting to be let go. If you hold the wheels so they can't spin, it doesn't *hurt* the toy, but the whole thing is just vibrating in your hand, right? It's just fighting you, wanting to spin. That’s my legs. They just want to go. They *need* to go. And if I don’t let them, if I force them to stay still like in that dentist chair I was telling you about, that’s when it gets frustrating. Not painful, just... maddening, really. It's this overwhelming physical demand to just move, and you just can't ignore it, no matter how hard you try to tune it out. So yeah, no pain, just... a motor that won't shut off.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever tried taking any medications—prescription or over-the-counter—to help with this restless feeling in your legs, and if so, did any of them actually help?\n\nPatient: Well, no, I haven’t. I mean, it never really crossed my mind to take a pill for something like this, to be completely honest with you. Like I said before, I’ll take an ibuprofen or maybe an Aleve if my knee is really acting up after a long shift of buffing the hallways, because that’s an actual ache, right? You go to the pharmacy, you see a hundred different bottles for pain or headaches or allergies. But I’ve never exactly seen a box on the shelf at Walgreens that says, you know, \"for when your legs feel like a wind-up toy.\"\n\nI just always figured it was just... me. Just how I’m wired. Too much energy, or a motor running hot. How do you even medicate that anyway? What kind of pill just stops your legs from wanting to move without knocking you out completely? I can't be taking sleeping pills or something that makes me drowsy, I've got a whole building to maintain! If a pipe bursts in the second-floor men's room at 2 PM, I can't be groggy. I have to be sharp, ready to grab the wrench and get to work. \n\nSo, no, prescription, over-the-counter, vitamins, whatever... I haven't tried any of it for the restlessness. My wife, she’s always trying to get me to drink chamomile tea or cut back on my coffee, thinking maybe it's the caffeine making me jittery. But I've been drinking the exact same thermos of Folgers dark roast every single morning since about 1992, and let me tell you, it doesn't make a lick of difference whether I have one cup or four. The buzzing is just there the second I sit down, coffee or no coffee. \n\nSo I guess my only \"medicine\" for it is just... getting up and doing something. Grabbing a broom, pacing the room, doing a lap around the gymnasium to check the bleachers. That's the only thing that actually helps. But as far as actual medication goes? No, never tried anything. Never even asked a doctor about it before today, honestly. I just didn't think there was anything in a bottle that could fix it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I absolutely have to let that energy out somehow, even if it's just tapping my heel against the bottom of the chair, otherwise I feel like I'm going to literally jump out of my skin.\n\nDoctor: When you describe that buzzing feeling and the pressure behind your kneecaps, can you tell me more about what that sensation actually feels like? Is it uncomfortable or painful, like tingling or burning? Or is it more just an urge or drive to move without any particular uncomfortable feeling?\n\nPatient: Pain? No, no, it’s definitely not pain. I mean, I know what pain feels like, believe me. You spend thirty-something years walking on polished concrete and terrazzo floors, pushing heavy equipment around, and your joints are absolutely going to let you know about it. I’ve got a bit of normal wear and tear, maybe a little arthritis in my left knee—flares up when it rains, or when the barometer drops, you know how that goes. That’s a real ache. That’s a sharp, physical pain where you want to take an ibuprofen and put a bag of frozen peas on it. \n\nBut this buzzing thing? It’s completely different. It doesn't hurt at all. And it’s not a tingle, either. It’s not like when you sit on your foot wrong and it falls asleep, and you get those horrible pins and needles when the blood starts rushing back. It’s nothing like that. No burning, no numbness, nothing that would make you think a nerve is pinched or something. \n\nIt really is just... exactly what you said a second ago. An urge. A drive. It’s like... well, it’s like my legs just woke up and decided it’s time to run a marathon, even if the rest of my brain is just trying to watch the local weather report. There’s no hurt to it, it’s just this intense, coiled-up energy. \n\nThink of it like a wind-up toy, you know? You twist that little metal key on the side until it won’t turn anymore, and you can feel the tension in the spring just waiting to be let go. If you hold the wheels so they can't spin, it doesn't *hurt* the toy, but the whole thing is just vibrating in your hand, right? It's just fighting you, wanting to spin. That’s my legs. They just want to go. They *need* to go. And if I don’t let them, if I force them to stay still like in that dentist chair I was telling you about, that’s when it gets frustrating. So yeah, no pain, just... a motor that won't shut off.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever tried taking any medications—prescription or over-the-counter—to help with this restless feeling in your legs, and if so, did any of them actually help?\n\nPatient: Well, no, I haven’t. I can't be taking sleeping pills or something that makes me drowsy, I've got a whole building to maintain! If a pipe bursts in the second-floor men's room at 2 PM, I can't be groggy. I have to be sharp, ready to grab the wrench and get to work. \n\nSo, no, prescription, over-the-counter, vitamins, whatever... I haven't tried any of it for the restlessness. My wife, she’s always trying to get me to drink chamomile tea or cut back on my coffee, thinking maybe it's the caffeine making me jittery. But I've been drinking the exact same thermos of Folgers dark roast every single morning since about 1992, and let me tell you, it doesn't make a lick of difference whether I have one cup or four. The buzzing is just there the second I sit down, coffee or no coffee. \n\nSo I guess my only \"medicine\" for it is just... getting up and doing something. Well, see, that’s... I mean, I guess I’ve always been a guy who likes to keep moving, but this really intense stretch? Where it’s just constantly buzzing and I can’t sit down for five minutes without feeling like I need to jump up? That’s been going on for about a year and five months now. 

I know exactly how long it’s been because that’s right when the district brought in this new facilities management company, and they completely overhauled everything. Before that, I had my set routine, you know? I worked my standard shift, knew exactly what hallways I was buffing, knew exactly when I was taking my fifteen-minute break in the boiler room. I had a rhythm. 

But then this new company comes in, and they changed my cleaning contract entirely. Suddenly, I’m on rotating hours. One week I’m doing the early morning shift, getting there at 5 AM to unlock the doors and check the HVAC systems, and the next week they’ve got me on evenings, closing up the building at midnight. Your body just doesn't know what to do with that. 

And on top of the rotating shifts, they started piling on all this extra event work. It used to be that just a couple of guys handled the after-school stuff, but now we all have to rotate through it. So now I’m setting up hundreds of folding chairs for choir concerts, or I’m waiting around in the back hall for a regional basketball tournament to finish up so I can go in and sweep up all the popcorn and sticky soda spills from the bleachers. And let me tell you, those crowds make an absolute mess, it takes hours to get the gymnasium floor looking decent again, especially getting the scuff marks off the hardwood. 

Because my schedule is just all over the place now, I feel like my internal clock is completely shot. I’ll be sitting in the breakroom at 8:30 at night, just trying to eat a turkey sandwich while I wait for a PTA meeting to wrap up so I can lock the library, and my legs are just going a mile a minute. I can't even relax for the ten minutes I have to myself. So, yeah, I'd say it's been about a year and five months of this worse stretch, right when that contract changed and they started throwing all these random hours and events at me. I mean, I actually tried talking to the union rep about the schedule, just to see if I could get back on a straight day shift, but you know how that goes, it's just a lot of red tape and paperwork, and in the meantime, I'm just vibrating in my chair waiting for the next bell to ring.

It’s like... well, it’s like my legs just woke up and decided it’s time to run a marathon, even if the rest of my brain is just trying to watch the local weather report. There’s no hurt to it, it’s just this intense, coiled-up energy. \n\nThink of it like a wind-up toy, you know? You twist that little metal key on the side until it won’t turn anymore, and you can feel the tension in the spring just waiting to be let go. If you hold the wheels so they can't spin, it doesn't *hurt* the toy, but the whole thing is just vibrating in your hand, right? It's just fighting you, wanting to spin. That’s my legs. They just want to go. They *need* to go. And if I don’t let them, if I force them to stay still like in that dentist chair I was telling you about, that’s when it gets frustrating. Not painful, just... maddening, really. It's this overwhelming physical demand to just move, and you just can't ignore it, no matter how hard you try to tune it out. So yeah, no pain, just... a motor that won't shut off.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever tried taking any medications—prescription or over-the-counter—to help with this restless feeling in your legs, and if so, did any of them actually help?\n\nPatient: Well, no, I haven’t. I mean, it never really crossed my mind to take a pill for something like this, to be completely honest with you. Like I said before, I’ll take an ibuprofen or maybe an Aleve if my knee is really acting up after a long shift of buffing the hallways, because that’s an actual ache, right? You go to the pharmacy, you see a hundred different bottles for pain or headaches or allergies. But I’ve never exactly seen a box on the shelf at Walgreens that says, you know, \"for when your legs feel like a wind-up toy.\"\n\nI just always figured it was just... me. Just how I’m wired. Too much energy, or a motor running hot. How do you even medicate that anyway? What kind of pill just stops your legs from wanting to move without knocking you out completely? I can't be taking sleeping pills or something that makes me drowsy, I've got a whole building to maintain! If a pipe bursts in the second-floor men's room at 2 PM, I can't be groggy. I have to be sharp, ready to grab the wrench and get to work. \n\nSo, no, prescription, over-the-counter, vitamins, whatever... I haven't tried any of it for the restlessness. My wife, she’s always trying to get me to drink chamomile tea or cut back on my coffee, thinking maybe it's the caffeine making me jittery. But I've been drinking the exact same thermos of Folgers dark roast every single morning since about 1992, and let me tell you, it doesn't make a lick of difference whether I have one cup or four. The buzzing is just there the second I sit down, coffee or no coffee. \n\nSo I guess my only \"medicine\" for it is just... getting up and doing something. Grabbing a broom, pacing the room, doing a lap around the gymnasium to check the bleachers. That's the only thing that actually helps. But as far as actual medication goes? No, never tried anything. Never even asked a doctor about it before today, honestly. I just didn't think there was anything in a bottle that could fix it.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this restless, buzzing feeling in your legs? Has it been going on for months, years, or can you remember roughly when it started?\n\nPatient: Well, see, that’s... I mean, I guess I’ve always been a guy who likes to keep moving, but this really intense stretch? Where it’s just constantly buzzing and I can’t sit down for five minutes without feeling like I need to jump up? That’s been going on for about a year and five months now. \n\nI know exactly how long it’s been because that’s right when the district brought in this new facilities management company, and they completely overhauled everything. Before that, I had my set routine, you know? I worked my standard shift, knew exactly what hallways I was buffing, knew exactly when I was taking my fifteen-minute break in the boiler room. I had a rhythm. \n\nBut then this new company comes in, and they changed my cleaning contract entirely. Suddenly, I’m on rotating hours. One week I’m doing the early morning shift, getting there at 5 AM to unlock the doors and check the HVAC systems, and the next week they’ve got me on evenings, closing up the building at midnight. Your body just doesn't know what to do with that. \n\nAnd on top of the rotating shifts, they started piling on all this extra event work. It used to be that just a couple of guys handled the after-school stuff, but now we all have to rotate through it. So now I’m setting up hundreds of folding chairs for choir concerts, or I’m waiting around in the back hall for a regional basketball tournament to finish up so I can go in and sweep up all the popcorn and sticky soda spills from the bleachers. And let me tell you, those crowds make an absolute mess, it takes hours to get the gymnasium floor looking decent again, especially getting the scuff marks off the hardwood. \n\nBecause my schedule is just all over the place now, I feel like my internal clock is completely shot. I’ll be sitting in the breakroom at 8:30 at night, just trying to eat a turkey sandwich while I wait for a PTA meeting to wrap up so I can lock the library, and my legs are just going a mile a minute. I can't even relax for the ten minutes I have to myself. So, yeah, I'd say it's been about a year and five months of this worse stretch, right when that contract changed and they started throwing all these random hours and events at me. I mean, I actually tried talking to the union rep about the schedule, just to see if I could get back on a straight day shift, but you know how that goes, it's just a lot of red tape and paperwork, and in the meantime, I'm just vibrating in my chair waiting for the next bell to ring.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
